## Page 1


Unveiling universality, encloseness, and
orthogonality in dynamics
J. Aaronson, A. I. Danilenko∗, J. Kułaga-Przymus, M. Lemańczyk
with an appendix “Separable subsets for d-bar metric”
by T. Austin
April 24, 2026
Abstract
Motivated by Sarnak’s conjecture on Möbius orthogonality, we in-
vestigate the general problem of orthogonality for a bounded sequence
to topological models of characteristic classes of measure-preserving
automorphisms. Our main observation is that whenever a strong form
of such orthogonality holds in a system pX, Tq then the orthogonality
holds for all topological systems in which each ergodic measure yields
an automorphism that is measure-theoretically isomorphic to one aris-
ing from an ergodic measure in pX, Tq. This leads us to study two
purely dynamical problems. The first concerns the existence of univer-
sal topological models for characteristic classes of measure-preserving
automorphisms.
In particular, we show that the class of automor-
phisms with relative discrete spectrum over the identity factor–as well
as several related classes including the weakly mixing case–admit such
models. The second problem we address is the existence of a common
ergodic extension for a measurable family of ergodic automorphisms,
with particular emphasis on the role of ergodic decomposition.
We
solve this problem by analysing separable subsets in d-bar distance.
We also highlight potential applications to the orthogonality phenom-
ena, especially those connected to Sarnak’s conjecture and its loga-
rithmic variant. In particular, we provide a purely ergodic proof of the
equivalence—first established by T. Tao—between the local 1-Fourier
uniformity of a sequence u: N Ñ D and the vanishing of its second
Gowers–Host–Kra seminorm, namely }u}u2 “ 0. Moreover, we show
that if the set of all measure-theoretic eigenvalues of a zero entropy
system pX, Tq is countable, then pX, Tq satisfies Sarnak’s conjecture
along a subsequence of full logarithmic density.
∗A.I.D. was supported in part by the “Long-term program of support of the Ukrainian
research teams at the Polish Academy of Sciences carried out in collaboration with the
U.S. National Academy of Sciences with the financial support of external partners”.

Theorem A. Assume that pX, Tq satisfies the strong u-MOMO property.
Let pZ, Rq be an arbitrary topological system such that for each ν P M epZ, Rq
there is an ergodic measure ν1 P MepX, Tq for which the measure-theoretic
automorphisms pZ, ν, Rq and pX, ν1, Tq are isomorphic. Then pZ, Rq K u.
The same result holds in the logarithmic case.
Theorem A suggests to study the problem of existence of universal models
for a characteristic class (which is of independent interest in dynamics).
Given a class F of measure-preserving automorphisms8, a universal model
for F is a topological system pX, Tq such that:
— for every ν P MpX, Tq, the measure-theoretic system pX, ν, Tq belongs
to F; and
— for every automorphism R P F there exists a measure ν P MpX, Tq
such that pX, ν, Tq is isomorphic to R.
We recall that classically, the class ALL of all automorphisms has a
universal model (for example, the full shift with alphabet r0, 1s),9 while
Serafin’s theorem [63] tells us that the class ZE has no such a model. It is
not hard to observe (see Proposition 5.1) that if a characteristic class F has
a universal model then it has to be an ec-class: F “ Fec. Due to Theorem A,
proving CF K u is equivalent to showing the strong u-MOMO property for
one of the universal models of F (assuming there is a such). As already
mentioned, the present challenge (on the way to prove Sarnak’s conjecture
in full generality) is to establish CDISPec K µ. A difficulty stems from the
fact that for systems in CDISPec, non-ergodic measures may yield measure-
theoretic automorphisms with continuous (possibly Lebesgue) spectrum in
the orthocomplement of the L2-space associated with the sigma-algebra of
invariant sets, so the aforementioned spectral disjointness argument (see (3)
and Footnote 4) does not work. In the light of Serafin’s result, the following
theorem looks rather surprising (T stands for the circle, we use both additive
and multiplicative notations; which one is used should be clear from the
context).
to prove that it enjoys the strong µ-MOMO property can be a considerable task. For
example, the Möbius orthogonality of horocycle flows has been known since [9], however,
their strong µ-MOMO property remains an open problem. Moreover, the reader is warned
that it is not true that whenever pX, Tq K u then the system satisfies the strong u-MOMO
property. Indeed, in [13], it is shown that there are positive entropy systems orthogonal
to µ, but on the other hand, no positive entropy system can enjoy the strong u-MOMO
property (assuming the Chowla conjecture) [41].

Recall that the main result of [41] asserts that CFec K u is equivalent to
the so called Veech condition for all Furstenberg systems of u (see Section 1.3
for the notion of Furstenberg system of a bounded sequence). For the class
CDISPec the Veech condition is the vanishing of the second Gowers-Host-Kra
seminorm: }u}u2 “ 0, see [41]. There are some natural subclasses
CDISPpΛqec Ă CDISPec,

its Lebesgue measure is zero and this implies that the maximal spectral type
of the automorphisms associated to all invariant measures have singular

the logarithmic Sarnak’s conjecture holds for our systems:

## Page 9


The critical property here is to have a well-defined “joining” of possibly
uncountable set of ergodic components pR, νγq. It raises one more natural
problem which is of independent interest in dynamics. Namely, we want to
study non-ergodic systems which are close to ergodic ones in the sense that
they are factors of the systems of the form IdˆErgodic. As we will see in
Appendix, rather surprisingly, the problem relates the existence of a common
extension for ergodic components with the separability in d-bar distance.16
The following is a sample of consequences of results proved in Appendix.
Theorem G (see the proof in Section 3.6, cf. Proposition 3.1(B)). Let
pX, ν, Tq be an automorphism and let ν “
ş
Γ νγ dPpγq denote its ergodic
decomposition. The following are equivalent:
(i) T is a factor of an automorphism of the form Id ˆ Ergodic.
(ii) There is an ergodic automorphism pY, κ, Sq such that for P-a.e. γ P Γ,
the automorphism pT, νγq is a factor of pY, κ, Sq.
(iii) There is an automorphism pY, κ, Sq such that for P-a.e. γ P Γ, the
automorphism pT, νγq is a factor of pY, κ, Sq.
We call systems satisfying (i) (or (ii) or (iii)) uniformly relatively ergodic
(URE) or enclosed 17. A topological dynamical system is topologically URE
if each invariant measure yields a URE system. Some examples and other
basic properties of enclosed automorphisms and topological URE systems
are presented in Section 3. An extension of the notion of URE to Markov
URE and piecewise Markov URE is also discussed in Section 3.
Returning to possible contributions toward a better understanding of
the orthogonality problem, we obtain the following (UE below stands for the
class of uniquely ergodic systems).

u.18 Then, for every topological system pX, Tq in CFec for which all visible
measures19 are enclosed, we have pX, Tq K u.

Denote by CT the class of topological dynamical systems with countably

We recall that the assumption of Corollary H (for F=ZE) is satisfied
for the logarithmic orthogonality and u “ µ by the break-through result of
Frantzikinakis and Host [22] and we obtain that the zero entropy topologi-
cally URE systems satisfy logarithmic Sarnak’s conjecture. In fact, as proved
in [22], the Pinsker factors of logarithmic Furstenberg systems of µ are distal,
so we can also apply the second part of the corollary for the class G “ DIST
of distal automorphisms to obtain that the logarithmic Sarnak’s conjecture
holds for all topological systems whose distal factors of automorphisms given
by visible measures are enclosed. However, this particular result has already
a recent strengthening by Huang, Tan and Xu [34] to topological systems
in which for each invariant measure, the Kronecker factors of a.a. of its er-
godic components have a common extension (they call this property almost
countable spectrum). In Section 8.1, we prove that, in fact, the property
of almost countability does not depend on the choice of negligible subsets
for all invariant measures: systems with almost countable spectrum are pre-
cisely those that have countable measure-theoretic spectrum (i.e. the union
ΛpX, Tq of the sets of eigenvalues of pX, ν, Tq, ν P M epX, Tq, is countable).
For completeness, we state a refined version of the main result from [34] and
provide a shorter proof of it in Section 8.2.
So far, thinking about applications, we mainly concentrated on finding
properties of topological systems that are sufficient for the orthogonality with
u. However, another option is to focus on ergodic properties of Fursten-
berg systems of u themselves. This will ease (via some disjointness type
argument) either proving orthogonality with all systems from CF or with
a certain subclass. Corollary I below provides a sample result that can be
deduced that way.
In the context of the logarithmic Sarnak’s conjecture, we recall a theorem
of Frantzikinakis [21]: If all logarithmic Furstenberg systems of the Möbius
function µ are ergodic, then the logarithmic Sarnak’s conjecture holds. Our
goal is to extend this result by relaxing the ergodicity assumption, replacing
it with suitable conditions on the (measurable) families of ergodic compo-
nents of Furstenberg systems associated with µ (see Section 1.2 for the notion
of piecewise Markov URE).
20See Section 1.1 for the definition of AG.
10


## Page 11


Corollary I. Logarithmic Sarnak’s conjecture holds if and only if for each
logarithmic Furstenberg system pXµ, κ, Sq, the corresponding Pinsker factor
is piecewise Markov URE. In particular, the logarithmic Sarnak’s conjecture
holds provided that, for the Pinsker factor of each Furstenberg system of µ,
its a.a. ergodic components have a common extension.
1.1
Universal models for characteristic classes
Recall that a class F of measure-preserving automorphisms is a character-
istic class if it is closed under taking (countable) joinings21 and factors.22
From now on, F will always stand for a characteristic class. Every auto-
morphism R acting on a standard probability space pZ, D, κq possesses a
largest (and hence unique) factor lying in F [50, 59]; we denote this fac-
tor by AFpRq.23 The present paper takes first steps toward understanding
which characteristic classes admit universal models, and, in particular, we
show that non-trivial such classes do exist (e.g. Theorems B and E). It turns
out (see Proposition 5.1 below) that if F has a universal model then F must

factors of all countable self-joinings of T). For example, in the weak mixing
case in Theorem 1.13 we prove that the characteristic class generated by an
MSJ automorphism24 admits a universal model, see Section 1.4 for precise
results.
1.2
(Markov) Uniform Relative Ergodicity
An important characterization of the URE property in terms of Ornstein’s
d-bar distance is done in Appendix, Corollary 10.4:
Theorem 1.1. T is URE (enclosed) if and only if after removing a subset of
measure zero of ergodic components, the remaining ergodic components yield
a separable subset in the d-bar metric.
24An ergodic automorphism T has the minimal self-joining property (MSJ) [39, 59] if
all its ergodic (infinite) self-joinings are Cartesian products of off-diagonal joinings, the
latter being obtained as the images of µ under the maps
X Q x ÞÑ px, T i1x, T i2x, . . .q P X8
with i1, i2, . . . arbitrary integers. For weakly mixing MSJ T, we have T m K T n whenever
m ‰ n [39]. Moreover, such automorphisms enjoy the PID property, that is, for any other
automorphisms R, S and any ρ a joining of T, R and S which is pairwise independent, ρ
is the product measure.
12


## Page 13


From this theorem we deduce a characterization of the topological URE.
The following statements are equivalent (see Corollary 3.20):
— a topological system pX, Tq is topologically URE,
— the set M epX, Tq of ergodic T-invariant measures is d-bar separable,
— all measures from M epX, Tq yield automorphisms that have a common
extension.
Many consequences of the URE are provided in Section 3. We mention
here only one of them (Proposition 3.1(C)):
If T is URE and T P Fec for a characteristic class F then T P F.
The URE property is also related to the recent paper [31] in which the
orthogonality of u to all uniquely ergodic systems: u K UE, is studied (this
problem is attributed to M. Boshernitzan in [10]). If u is such, it is not
hard to see that there cannot be κ P V puq which yields a non-trivial ergodic
Furstenberg system (see Section 1.3 for notation V puq). In fact, in [31], it
is proved that u K UE if and only if for each κ P V puq the function π025 is
orthogonal to all ergodic Markov quasi-images of L2pX, µq:

1.4

While, by its very definition, the strong u-MOMO property is combinatorial,
actually, it is a purely dynamical concept. In fact, a joining flavour of the
strong u-MOMO property can be seen in the following (by A we denote
te2πij{3 : j “ 0, 1, 2u):
Proposition 1.4. Let pX, Tq be a topological system. Then it satisfies the
strong u-MOMO property if and only if for all ν P MpX ˆ A, T ˆ IdAq,
κ P V puq and all joinings ρ P JppT ˆ IdA, νq, pS, κqq, we have

Although this proposition is a rather direct consequence of the Lifting
Lemma from [41], we provide a proof for completeness.
This joining approach is often used to establish new results on the sta-
bility of u-orthogonality in different topological models of a given measure-
preserving automorphism as seen in the proofs of most of our main results.

2.2
Isomorphisms and factors of non-ergodic automorphisms
We first recall the following classical result (see e.g. [44, Theorem 18.1]) that
will be utilized repeatedly in this paper.
Theorem 2.2. (Jankov-von Neumann Theorem) Let X, Y be standard Borel
spaces and f : X Ñ Y a Borel map. Then, fpXq is analytic and there exists
a measurable selector s: pfpXq, ApfpXqq Ñ pX, BpXqq, f ˝s “ IdfpXq, where
ApfpXqq denotes the sigma-algebra generated by analytic sets.
Assume now that T P AutpX, B, µq, and let IpTq “ Invµ denote the

pX, B, µq :“ pX, B, µq{IpTq,

stand for the corresponding disintegration. It is called the ergodic decomposi-
tion of µ. It follows from the uniqueness of disintegration that the conditional
measures µx are T-invariant. Besides, they are ergodic. A suggestive picture
for the ergodic decomposition appears when we assume additionally that T
is aperiodic (i.e. the measure of periodic points equals zero). Then there is
a standard nonatomic probability space pY, C, νq and a measurable mapping
X Q x ÞÑ Tx P AutpY, C, νq such that:
(a) pX, B, µq “ pX, B, µq b pY, C, νq,
(b) Tx is ergodic for µ-a.e. x P X, i.e. X Q x ÞÑ Tx P EpY, C, νq,
(c) the action T is isomorphic to Tpx, yq “ px, Tx yq on the product space
pX ˆ Y, µ b νq.
In other words, T is relatively ergodic over the first coordinate sigma-algebra
(i.e. the sigma-algebra of invariant sets). In the general case, the space X of
ergodic components is partitioned into X “ X1 \ X2 \ . . . \ X8, with Xk
being the set of points with (the smallest) period k, where the action of T
(represented by Tx) on the ergodic components for x P Xk are rotations by 1
(mod k) on the finite set t0, 1, . . . , k´1u equipped with the equidistribution,
and the aperiodic part is represented by X8 ˆ Y as above.

The following lemma is well known. For the completeness of our argument
we provide a short proof of it.
Lemma 2.8. Let pX, µ, Tq be a probability preserving dynamical system.
Then there is a topological system pY, Sq and a probability measure ν on Y
such that pX, µ, Tq is isomorphic to pY, S, νq.

2.4
Joinings of non-ergodic automorphisms
Given two dynamical systems pX, B, µ, Tq and pY, C, ν, Sq, a joining of them
is a pT ˆ Sq-invariant measure on the product space X ˆ Y such that the
marginal projections of ρ to X to Y are µ and ν respectively. The dynamical
system pX ˆ Y, B b C, ρ, T ˆ Sq is also called a joining of pX, B, µ, Tq and
pY, C, ν, Sq. The set of all joinings of T and S is denoted by JpT, Sq. Given
ρ P JpT, Sq, a linear operator Φρ : L2pX, µq Ñ L2pY, νq is well defined by

each f, g P L2pX, B, µq

3
On the URE property
3.1
Basic properties and examples of URE automorphisms
Prior to an overview of examples and counterexamples let us give some gen-
eral properties of the class of uniformly relatively ergodic automorphisms.
First of all, notice that an automorphism is URE if and only if its aperiodic
part is URE, so in what follows we tacitly assume that we consider only the
aperiodic case.
Proposition 3.1.
(A) The class of URE automorphisms is closed under
taking factors.
(B) Let T be an automorphism. The following are equivalent.
(i) T is URE.
(ii) There is a set of full measure of ergodic components of T having
a common ergodic extension.
(iii) There is a countable set of ergodic components pT, µxiq, i ě 1 and
ρ P JeppT, µx1q, pT, µx2q, . . .q such that T is a factor of the product
Id ˆ pś
iě1pT, µxiq, ρq.
(C) If T P Fec and T is URE then T P F.
Proof. (A): This is obvious.
(B): (ii) ñ (i) This implication follows from Corollary 2.7.
(i) ñ (ii) This follows from the fact that ergodic components of a factor
are isomorphic to factors of ergodic components.
(i) ñ (iii) (This is standard and follows from the separability of the space
pB, µq, but we provide a proof for completeness.) We need to prove that given
an automorphism R acting on pZ, D, κq and a family of factors Ei Ă D, i P I,
generating D, we can find a countable I0 Ă I such that Ž
iPI0 Ei “ D. For
this aim, note that S :“ Ť
iPI Ei is closed under taking complement, so the
semi-ring rS generated by S consists of finite intersections of elements from
S. Then the corresponding algebra Ap rSq consists of finite disjoint unions of
sets from rS. Since the space pD, κq (sets are considered modulo measure κ)
is separable, we can choose a countable subset tFjujě1 dense in Ap rSq. By
the form of the sets Fj, it follows that there exists a countable set I0 Ă I
such that Fj P Ž
iPI0 Ei for all j ě 1. Since D is the closure of Ap rSq, the set
tFj : j ě 1u is also dense in pD, κq and the claim follows.
(iii) ñ (i) This follows straightforwardly from the definition of URE.
(C): This follows from (B)(iii) since T P Fec is a factor of Id ˆ R, where
R “ pś
iě1pT, µxiq, ρq for an ergodic joining ρ of a family of T-ergodic com-
ponents pT, µxiq P F, i “ 1, 2, . . . . Hence, R P F. This implies, in turn, that
T P F.

Example 3.2. Denote by T “ tTiuiPI the family of ergodic components of
an automorphism T of a standard probability space.
(i) If |T | ď ℵ0 then T is URE (consider any ergodic joining of Tis).
(ii) If T is uncountable and pairwise disjoint then T cannot be URE.41
Example 3.3. Let each ergodic component of an automorphism pX, µ, Tq
is Bernoulli. Then T is URE. Indeed, each ergodic component is a factor of
the Bernoulli shift of infinite entropy.

3.3
Non-URE extensions and Cartesian products of URE-
automorphisms
The URE property is a generalization of the countablity of the set of ergodic
components. However, despite the fact that a finite extension of an automor-
phism with countably many ergodic components still have countably many
ergodic components and, hence, is URE, a finite extension of an arbitrary
URE automorphism need not be URE as the following example shows.
Example 3.10. Let T be an ergodic discrete spectrum automorphism on
pX, µq. Assume that for each ω P Ω:“ t0, 1uN, there is a cocycle ϕω for T
with values in Z{2Z such that:
— the mapping ΩQ ω ÞÑ ϕω is measurable,
— the cocycle ϕω is ergodic for each ω,
— the skew product extensions Tϕω and Tϕω1 are not isomorphic if the
sequences ω and ω1 are not tail equivalent.43
Let λ denote the Bernoulli measure t0.5, 0.5ubN on Ω.
Consider the
dynamical system pΩˆ X, λ b µ, Id ˆ Tq. By definition, it is URE. On the
other hand, consider the following 2-point extension R of Id ˆ T acting on
pΩˆ X ˆ Z{2Z, λ b µ b λ2q (with λ2 being the p0.5, 0.5q-measure) of this
system:
Rpω, x, kq :“ pω, Tx, k ` ϕωpxqq.
We claim that R is not URE. Indeed, the space of ergodic components of
R is pΩ, λq. For each ω P Ω, the ergodic component of R corresponding to
ω is the ϕω-skew product extension Tϕω of T. Take two points ω and ω1
of Ω. If they are not tail equivalent then Tϕω and Tϕω1 are not isomorphic.
It follows from Lemma 3.11 below that Tϕω and Tϕω1 are relatively disjoint
over the common factor T in a strong sense. Therefore, if there exists an
ergodic transformation S on a standard probability space pY, νq containing
all systems pX ˆ Z{2Z, Tϕω, µ b λ2q, ω P Ω, as factors then L2pY, νq contains
L2pX ˆ Z{2Z, µ b λ2q as closed subspaces for all ω P Ω. Since T has pure
point spectrum, pX, µ, Tq is a common factor for all dynamical systems pX ˆ
Z{2Z, Tϕω, µ b λ2q inside pY, νq.
Thus, we obtain that the Hilbert space
L2pY, νq contains uncountably many closed subspaces L2pX ˆ Z{2Z, µ b
λ2q each of which includes a common subspace L2pX, µq.
Moreover, the
orthocomplements L2pX ˆ Z{2Z, µ b λ2q a L2pX, µq are pairwise orthogonal
in L2pY, νq by Lemma 3.11 below. Hence, L2pY, νq would not be separable,
a contradiction.
43For a concrete example of such a situation, see e.g. [46, §4], (Kakutani’s examples).

3.6
URE via the d-bar, and proof of Theorem G
In this subsection we will use the d-bar distance to show the URE property
for some classes of dynamical systems. First, we prove Theorem G.
Proof of Theorem F. In view of Proposition 3.1(B), it remains to prove only
the hard part (iii)ñ(ii). By Lemma 10.2, the set of factors of pY, κ, Sq is
sd-separable. (It is not assumed in that lemma that S is ergodic.) Hence
the set of ergodic components pT, νγq, γ P Γ, is sd-separable (upon deleting a
subset of zero measure, if necessary). On the other hand, each sd-separable
subset of ergodic dynamical systems has a common ergodic extension by
Corollary 10.4.

## Page 45


From Appendix and Proposition 3.19 we deduce the following character-
ization of the TURE (topological URE) property for topological systems.
Corollary 3.20. The following are equivalent:
(i) πZ
˚pMepX, Tqq is d-separable.
(ii) For each measure µ P MpX, Tq, the dynamical system pX, µ, Tq is
URE.
(iii) There exists a dynamical system pY, κ, Rq such that pX, µ, Tq is a factor
of pY, κ, Rq for each µ P MepX, Tq.
(iv) There exists an ergodic dynamical system pY, κ, Rq such that pX, µ, Tq
is a factor of pY, κ, Rq for each µ P MepX, Tq.
Proof. Proposition 10.3 gives the implication (i)ñ(iv). Proposition 3.19 and
Corollary 10.4 yield the implication (ii)ñ(i). The implication (iii)ñ(ii) fol-
lows from the definition of URE (see Theorem G(iii)). Of course, the impli-
cation (iv)ñ(iii) is trivial.

3.7
Remarks on the inverse limits of URE automorphisms
Remark 3.22. By Theorem 3.21, the inverse limit of a sequence of URE-
transformations is URE. Hence, the ergodic components of the inverse limit
have a common extension. However, Theorem 3.21 does not give any explicit
description of the common extension. We now provide such a description in

many ergodic components.

Remark 4.5. The shrinking wedge model is ubiquitous when we want to ex-
tend the u-orthogonality for uniquely ergodic systems to u-orthogonality of
topological systems having countably many ergodic measures (up to measure-
theoretic isomorphism). For example, let us prove that under the assump-
tion of Corollary H, we get the u-orthogonality for systems belonging to CFec
having countably many ergodic measures (up to measure-theoretic isomor-
phism). Indeed, first of all, the orthogonality of uniquely ergodic systems
extends to the strong u-MOMO property of such systems, cf. Footnote 18.
Take now pX, Tq P CFec with M epX, Tq, up to measure-theoretic isomor-
phism, equal to tνj : j ě 1u. Then find uniquely ergodic models pZj, κj, Rjq
for pT, νjq, j ě 1. Next, use the shrinking wedge construction to obtain a dy-
namical system pZ, Rq satisfying the strong u-MOMO property and having
as ergodic invariant measures κ1

jq is isomorphic to pZj, Rj, κjq,
j ě 1. Finally, use Theorem A.

4.4
Strong u-MOMO and Sarnak’s conjecture in topological
models: proofs of Theorem A, Theorem F and Corol-

Proof of Corollary H. Take pX, Tq satisfying the assumptions of Corollary H.
• By assumption, for each visible ν P MpX, Tq, the automorphism pT, νq
is a factor of Id ˆ E, with E ergodic.
• pX, Tq P CFec yields additionally that pT, νq is a factor of IdˆE1, where
E1 is ergodic and belongs to F.
• We can also assume that E1 is uniquely ergodic.
• Hence (by assumption), E1 K u, but in fact, due to [41], we can assume
that E1 satisfies the strong u-MOMO property.
• Hence, pT, νq is a measure-theoretic factor of Id ˆ E1, with this home-
omorphism satisfying strong u-MOMO property.
• This implies pX, Tq K u as in the proof of Theorem F.

5.3
Relatively discrete spectrum over identity
The concept of relatively discrete spectrum with respect to a factor is fun-
damental in ergodic theory [27], [69]. However, in almost all results on this
subject it is assumed that the factor relative to which we examine a given
automorphism is ergodic. In particular, the Halmos-von Neumann theorem
has been proved [69], [51] under such assumption. In this section, we will
consider exactly the opposite case, namely we study the relatively discrete
spectrum over the sigma-algebra of invariant sets.54
We start with recalling necessary definitions. Let T P AutpX, B, µq and
let A Ă B be a factor. We say that a measurable map f : X{A Ñ Upnq
(where Upnq stands for the group of unitary matrices of degree n) is a relative
eigenvalue if there exists a measurable F : X Ñ Cn such that:
(44)
fpxqFpxq “ FpTxq
for µ-a.e. x P X, where x ÞÑ x is the factor map
pX, B, µ, Tq Ñ pX{A, A, µ, Tq.
54In this situation the whole automorphism is relatively ergodic over the factor.

The following result explains when an automorphism with a relative dis-
crete spectrum (over the identity) has the smallest possible Kronecker factor.
Proposition 5.23. Assume that T P DISPec. Then the Kronecker factor
of T (which always contains IpTq) is equal to IpTq if and only if Tx K Tx1
for µ b µ-a.a. px, x1q P X ˆ X.
Proof. Suppose first that the Kronecker factor of T is IpTq. All we need to
show is that T K Erg [31]. So, suppose that T is not disjoint with an ergodic
automorphism R. As T P DISPec, there is a non-trivial joining of T with
ADISPecpRq. Since R is ergodic, ADISPecpRq “ ADISPpRq, i.e. ADISPecpRq
is the Kronecker factor of R. Then T has non-trivial Kronecker factor, a
contradiction.
Conversely, if the Kronecker factor of T contains non-trivial invariant
subsets, then there exist λ P Tzt1u and a nontrivial T-eigenfunction F such
that F ˝T “ λF. Denote by Rλ the rotation by λ on T. Then, the dynamical
system pT, F˚pµq, Rλq is a factor of T (via the mapping F). If λ is irrational
then pT, F˚pµq, Rλq is ergodic. If λ is rational and n is the least period of
λ, then it is always possible to choose F in such a way that F takes values
in the subgroup tz P T: zn “ 1u. Then pT, F˚pµq, Rλq is ergodic. Thus,
in every case, T has a nontrivial ergodic factor and, hence, T M Erg. This
implies that Tx M Tx1 for µ b µ-a.a. px, x1q P X ˆ X [31].

Corollary 5.33. Let T P DISPec. Then T P DISP if and only if T is
URE.
Proof. Let T P DISP. By Proposition 5.32, a.e. ergodic component of T
has discrete spectrum and ΛsxpTq Ă ΛpTq. Let R be an ergodic transfor-
mation with discrete spectrum such that ΛpRq Ą ΛpTq. Then a.e. ergodic
component of T is a factor of R. Hence, T is URE by Proposition 3.1.
Conversely, if T P DISPec and T is URE then T P DISP by Proposi-
tion 3.1(C).
The Corollary 5.33 implies, in turn, the following result (that has been
proved in [16] using different methods).
Corollary 5.34. A transformation T has pure discrete spectrum if and only
if there is a probability measure ν on T such that T is isomorphic to a factor
of the direct product I ˆ R, where I is the identity on pT, νq and R is an
ergodic transformation with a pure discrete spectrum and ΛpRq is the group
gr(ΛpTqq generated by ΛpTq.

Remark 6.4. Note that in the above lemma we proved the following, more
general, fact: if for an ergodic automorphism R, there are functions f, F (of
modulus 1) and β irrational such that f ˝R “ e2πiβf and F ˝R “ f ¨F then R
does not belong to FpA2,αq whenever α, β and 1 are rationally independent.
We now discuss some facts around Conjecture 9.7 from Section 9. We
say that an automorphism R acting on a space pZ, D, κq has quasi-discrete
spectrum of order 2 if there are non-zero functions F, f and β P R such that
f ˝ R “ e2πiβf, F ˝ R “ F ¨ f, and the set of all such F and f is total in
L2pZ, D, κq. Note that in this definition we can additionally assume that F
and f are bounded. We will call F a quasi-eigenfunction of T.
Lemma 6.5. If R has quasi-discrete spectrum of order 2, then for any λ P
J8pRq, we have pRˆ8, λq has also quasi-discrete spectrum of order 2.
Proof. Consider bounded fi and Fi and tensors of the form Â

If we additionally assume that R is ergodic, then we can additionally
assume that F and f have constant modulus 1.
Lemma 6.6. Assume that R is ergodic and has quasi-discrete spectrum of
order 2. If the spectrum of R is rational, then R has purely discrete spectrum.
Proof. We have f ˝ R “ e2πi{mf and then F ˝ R “ f ¨ F. It follows that
F ˝ Rm “ e2πipmpm´1q{2q{mfm ¨ F “ e2πipm´1q{2 ¨ F
since we can assume that f takes values in the group of roots of degree m
(in fact, by ergodicity, there is a unique Rokhlin tower A, RA, . . . , Rm´1A
fulfilling the whole space, and we can assume that f “ 1 on A, and then
f “ e2πik{m on RkA; in general we use the fact that the m-th power of f
is a constant). It follows that F is an eigenfunction of Rm, so its spectral
measure (for Rm) is Dirac, concentrated at e2πipm´1q{m. It follows that the
spectral measure of F for R must be discrete (as its image via the map
z ÞÑ zm is Dirac). Hence, all functions f, F have discrete spectral measures
and therefore the maximal spectral type of R is also discrete.
Lemma 6.7. Assume that R is ergodic and has quasi-discrete spectrum of
order 2. Let A Ă D be a factor of R with partly continuous spectrum. Then
there exist G, g P L2pAq (of constant modulus 1) such that G ˝ R “ g ¨ G,
g ˝ R “ e2πiβg and β is irrational.

Epf|Aq ‰ 0 then f must be equal to Epf|Aq up to a constant, hence, f is A-
measurable). Consider a quasi-eigenfunction F of RæA. Then F ˝ R “ f ¨ F

erated by an MSJ automorphism
Assume that T P AutpX, B, µq is an MSJ automorphism.
Our standing
assumption is that µ is nonatomic. This implies that T is weakly mixing.
We are interested in properties of the infinite Cartesian product T ˆ8 of T.
7.1
Characteristic class for simple automorphisms
Recall that, following [41], given an automorphism T, the smallest character-
istic class FpTq containing T consists of all factors of all infinite self-joinings
of T.
Assume that T P AutpX, B, µq is a simple automorphism60([39], [66]).
We recall that all ergodic discrete spectrum are simple. However, if a simple
automorphism has no purely discrete spectrum, it has to be weakly mixing.

Concluding remarks and open problems
Question 9.1. For which characteristic classes F, the product formula
ApFqpT ˆ Sq “ ApFqpTq b ApFqpSq
holds true?
It works for ZE, DISP and DIST for example but it breaks down al-
ready at the smallest (see [41]) nontrivial characteristic class ID (note that
ID “ IDec. Indeed, if T is ergodic then ApIDqpTq “ t˚u, so ApIDqpTq b
ApIDqpTq is also trivial, while ApIDqpT ˆ Tq is trivial if and only if T is
weakly mixing. We can also prove the following particular case.
Proposition 9.1. Let Fec be an ec-characteristic class. Assume that T P
AutpX, B, µq is ergodic and consider the identity IdY in AutpY, C, νq. Then,
we have
ApFecqpT ˆ IdY q “ ApFecqpTq b C.
Proof. Let D :“ ApFecqpT ˆ IdY q. Note that D Ą ApFecqpTq b C, in partic-
ular, it contains tH, Xu b C. It follows that we can apply Proposition 2.5,
and the factor D is given (by (18)) by a measurable choice
(67)
y ÞÑ Dy Ă B
of factors of T (i.e. of B). But (67) yields also the ergodic decomposition
of D. Since pT ˆ IdY q|D P Fec and we consider the Fec-class, the factors Dy
must be F-factors of T. Hence, all of them are contained in ApFecqpTq, and
therefore, D Ă ApFecqpTq b C.
Remark 9.2. We do not know if Proposition 9.1 works for any characteristic
class. It certainly goes beyond ec-classes, as it works for DISP, but also it
works for pqnq-rigidity classes F “ RIGppqnqq 73 as the following reasoning
shows.
Let F P L2pX ˆ Y, µ b νq satisfy F ˝ pT ˆ Idqqn Ñ F (in L2).
Then, for Fypxq :“ Fpx, yq we have Fy ˝ T qn Ñ Fy. It follows that when
F P L2pApFqpT ˆ Idqq then Fy P L2pApFqpTqq. The claim follows by the
following classical theorem: if A Ă B b C is a sigma-field such that Ay “ B
for a.e. y P Y and if A Ą tH, Xu b C then A “ B b C, see Proposition 2.1.

Appendix “Separable subsets for d-bar metric”
by T. Austin
In this note, the term ‘automorphism’ refers to an invertible measure-
preserving transformation on a standard Borel probability space, or to the
resulting measure-preserving system as a whole.
Let pK, dq be a nonempty compact metric space and let S be the leftward
shift on KZ. Let P be the space of S-invariant Borel probability measures
102


## Page 103


on KZ, and by default give it the vague topology, which is compact and
metrizable. The subset Pe of ergodic elements of P is Gδ in this topology,
and hence Borel.
The space P admits this generalization of Ornstein’s “d-bar” metric:
dpν, ν1q :“ inf
! ż

0q λpdy, dy1q : λ a joining of ν and ν1)

By vague compactness, this infimum is always attained. Furthermore, by
taking suitable ergodic components, it is always attained by an ergodic join-
ing if µ and ν are themselves ergodic. If K has at least two points, then d
generates a topology on P which is strictly stronger than the vague topology,
and which is neither compact nor separable. Other recent works using this
metric include [6, 7].
Intuitively, the relationship between d and the vague topology on P is
similar to the relationship between the ℓ8-metric and the product topology
on r0, 1sN.
Although d is strictly stronger than the weak topology, we do have the
following comparison.

We now use Proposition 10.3 to characterize the following property.
Definition 10.1. Let X and Y be automorphisms and assume that X is
ergodic. We say Y is enclosed by X if Y is a factor of W ˆ X for some
trivial automorphism W . An automorphism is enclosed if it is enclosed by
some ergodic automorphism.
In this definition, we can always take the space of W to be r0, 1s with
Lebesgue measure, but we do not need this restriction.

11.2.2
A Borel subgroup Λ Ă T and the Λ–Kronecker factor
Let Λ Ă T be a Borel subgroup. On each ergodic component pXu, κγ, Sq,
let KΛ
γ denote the maximal factor with discrete spectrum whose eigenvalue
group is contained in Λ (equivalently: its L2-space is the closed linear span
of eigenfunctions with eigenvalues in Λ). Let KΛ
rel denote the corresponding
relative Λ-Kronecker factor, see Lemma 5.24.
Proposition 11.6. For f “ π0 P L2pXu, κq the following are equivalent:
(i) π0 K L2`

σπ0,γptλuq ą 0 for some λ P Λ, then π0 has a nontrivial spectral atom at λ,
hence a nonzero projection onto the corresponding eigenspace, and therefore
π0 M L2pKΛ
γ q. Conversely, if σπ0,γptλuq “ 0 for all λ P Λ, then the spectral
measure of π0 has no point mass on Λ, so π0 is orthogonal to the span of all
eigenfunctions with eigenvalues in Λ, i.e. π0 K L2pKΛ
γ q.

Therefore, condition (ii) (applied to all Furstenberg systems κ P V puq) in
the above proposition is equivalent to the Veech condition (for u).
